## Specification (func.py as given)
```python
def count_special_permutations(A: int, B: int, M: int) -> int:
    """Count the number of permutations P of (1, 2, ..., AB-1) that satisfy:
    1. The length of a longest increasing subsequence of P is A
    2. The length of a longest decreasing subsequence of P is B  
    3. There exists an integer n such that appending n + 0.5 to the end of P 
       does not change either the length of a longest increasing subsequence 
       or the length of a longest decreasing subsequence
    
    Return the count modulo M.
    
    Args:
        A: Required length of longest increasing subsequence (A >= 2)
        B: Required length of longest decreasing subsequence (B >= 2)
        M: Modulus (prime number between 10^8 and 10^9)
    
    Returns:
        Number of valid permutations modulo M
    
    >>> count_special_permutations(3, 2, 998244353)
    10
    >>> count_special_permutations(10, 12, 924844033)
    623378361
    """
```

## Final func.py
```python
import math

def f_rect(A: int, B: int) -> int:
    """Number of standard Young tableaux of A x B rectangle (hook-length formula)."""
    N = A * B
    # Compute product of hook lengths
    prod = 1
    for i in range(B):
        for j in range(A):
            h = A - j + B - i - 1
            prod *= h
    return math.factorial(N) // prod


def catalan(n: int) -> int:
    """Catalan number C_n = binomial(2n, n) / (n+1)."""
    return math.comb(2 * n, n) // (n + 1)


def count_special_permutations(A: int, B: int, M: int) -> int:
    """Count the number of permutations P of (1, 2, ..., AB-1) that satisfy:
    1. The length of a longest increasing subsequence of P is A
    2. The length of a longest decreasing subsequence of P is B  
    3. There exists an integer n such that appending n + 0.5 to the end of P 
       does not change either the length of a longest increasing subsequence 
       or the length of a longest decreasing subsequence
    
    Return the count modulo M.
    
    Args:
        A: Required length of longest increasing subsequence (A >= 2)
        B: Required length of longest decreasing subsequence (B >= 2)
        M: Modulus (prime number between 10^8 and 10^9)
    
    Returns:
        Number of valid permutations modulo M
    
    >>> count_special_permutations(3, 2, 998244353)
    10
    >>> count_special_permutations(10, 12, 924844033)
    623378361
    """
    # The answer is:
    # ans = f_rect(A,B)^2 * C_{A-1} * C_{B-1} / C_{A+B-2}
    # where f_rect is the number of SYT of the A x B rectangle,
    # and C_n are Catalan numbers.
    
    # Compute f_rect(A,B) modulo M
    # Since f_rect can be huge, compute it modulo M using the product formula
    # f_rect(A,B) = (AB)! / prod(hooks)
    # We need this modulo M (prime), so we can use modular arithmetic.
    
    N = A * B
    # Compute numerator and denominator of f_rect modulo M
    # f_rect = N! / prod(hooks)
    # Compute N! mod M and product of hooks mod M, then multiply by modular inverse.
    
    num = 1
    for i in range(2, N + 1):
        num = (num * i) % M
    
    den = 1
    for i in range(B):
        for j in range(A):
            h = A - j + B - i - 1
            den = (den * h) % M
    
    f_mod = num * pow(den, -1, M) % M
    
    # Catalan numbers modulo M
    cA1 = catalan(A - 1) % M
    cB1 = catalan(B - 1) % M
    cAB2 = catalan(A + B - 2) % M
    
    ans = (f_mod * f_mod) % M
    ans = (ans * cA1) % M
    ans = (ans * cB1) % M
    ans = (ans * pow(cAB2, -1, M)) % M
    
    return ans
```

λ is contained in A×B, size is AB-1. The complement in the rectangle has exactly 1 cell. Wait, but what if λ is not contained in the A×B rectangle? Actually, λ₁ = A means row 1 has length A, so the shape extends to column A. λ₁' = B means column 1 has B rows, so the shape extends to row B. Actually, there could be more than B rows if row B+1 has length 0, but that's not counted. So exactly B rows with positive length, each at most A. So λ ⊆ A×B.

With |λ| = AB - 1, the only possibility is the rectangle minus (A,B).

Let's compute hook lengths generally.

For cell (i,j) where i ∈ [1, B], j ∈ [1, A]:

Hook length h(i,j) = arm + leg + 1.

For i < B, j < A:

leg = B - i
h = A - j + B - i + 1

For i = B, j < A:
arm = A - 1 - j
leg = 0
h = A - j

For i = B, j = A: cell doesn't exist.

 "command": "cd / && timeout 120 python3 << 'EOF'\n# Generate more data\ndef all_syt(shape):\n    N = sum(shape)\n    result = []\n    \n    def backtrack(cells, next_num):\n        if next_num > N:\n            rows = []\n            for r in range(len(shape)):\n                row = []\n                for c in range(shape[r]):\n                    row.append(cells[(r, c)])\n                rows.append(tuple(row))\n            result.append(tuple(rows))\n            return\n        \n        # Find all addable cells (outer corners)\n        for r in range(len(shape)):\n            for c in range(shape[r]):\n                if (r, c) in cells:\n                    continue\n                if (r > 0 and (r-1, c) not in cells):\n                    continue\n                if (c > 0 and (r, c-1) not in cells):\n                    continue\n                cells[(r, c)] = next_num\n                backtrack(cells, next_num + 1)\n                del cells[(r, c)]\n    \n    backtrack({}, 1)\n    return result\n\ndef shape_from_AB(A, B):\n    return [A] * (B - 1) + [A - 1]\n\ndef check_constraints(tableau, A, B):\n    for i in range(B - 1):\n        if not (tableau[i+1][A-2] < tableau[i][A-1]):\n            return False\n    if not (tableau[B-2][A-1] > tableau[B-1][A-2]):\n        return False\n    return True\n\nfor A, B in [(2,2),(2,3),(3,2),(2,4),(4,2),(3,3),(2,5),(5,2),(3,4),(4,3)]:\n    if A*B <= 13:\n        shape = shape_from_AB(A, B)\n        tabs = all_syt(shape)\n        f = len(tabs)\n        g = sum(1 for t in tabs if check_constraints(t, A, B))\n        print(f\"A={A}, B={B}: f={f}, g={g}, g*f={g*f}\")\nEOF"

{
 "command": "cd / && timeout 60 python3 << 'EOF'\n# Compute f_rect (number of SYT of full A x B rectangle)\ndef f_rect(A, B):\n    # Using hook-length formula\n    N = A * B\n    from math import factorial\n    hooks = []\n    for i in range(B):\n        for j in range(A):\n            # hook length = (A - j - 1) + (B - i - 1) + 1 = A - j + B - i - 1\n            h = A - j + B - i - 1\n            hooks.append(h)\n    prod = 1\n    for h in hooks:\n        prod *= h\n    return factorial(N) // prod\n\ndef all_syt(shape):\n    N = sum(shape)\n    result = []\n    \n    def backtrack(cells, next_num):\n        if next_num > N:\n            rows = []\n            for r in range(len(shape)):\n                row = []\n                for c in range(shape[r]):\n                    row.append(cells[(r, c)])\n                rows.append(tuple(row))\n            result.append(tuple(rows))\n            return\n        for r in range(len(shape)):\n            for c in range(shape[r]):\n                if (r, c) in cells:\n                    continue\n                if (r > 0 and (r-1, c) not in cells):\n                    continue\n                if (c > 0 and (r, c-1) not in cells):\n                    continue\n                cells[(r, c)] = next_num\n                backtrack(cells, next_num + 1)\n                del cells[(r, c)]\n    \n    backtrack({}, 1)\n    return result\n\ndef shape_from_AB(A, B):\n    return [A] * (B - 1) + [A - 1]\n\ndef check_constraints(tableau, A, B):\n    for i in range(B - 1):\n        if not (tableau[i+1][A-2] < tableau[i][A-1]):\n            return False\n    if not (tableau[B-2][A-1] > tableau[B-1][A-2]):\n        return False\n    return True\n\n# Compare f vs f_rect\nfor A, B in [(2,2),(2,3),(3,2),(2,4),(4,2),(3,3),(2,5),(5,2),(3,4),(4,3)]:\n    if A*B <= 13:\n        shape = shape_from_AB(A, B)\n        tabs = all_syt(shape)\n        f = len(tabs)\n        fr = f_rect(A, B)\n        print(f\"A={A}, B={B}: f={f}, f_rect={fr}, match={f==fr}\")\nEOF"
}

For the new cell to be (A,B), the insertion must end at row B, column A.

Row 1: x bumps P[1, A]. So P[1, A-1] < x < P[1, A].
Row 2: P[1, A] bumps P[2, A]. So P[2, A-1] < P[1, A] < P[2, A].

Row B-1: P[B-2, A] bumps P[B-1, A]. So P[B-1, A-1] < P[B-2, A] < P[B-1, A].
Row B: P[B-1, A] is placed at end of row B (column A, since row B has length A-1). So P[B-1, A] > P[B, A-1] (must be greater than all elements in row B, i.e., greater than P[B, A-1]).

For i = 1 to B-1: P[i+1, A-1] < P[i, A]

Actually, condition 3 says: there exists n such that appending n+0.5 doesn't change LIS or LDS. This is equivalent to: the permutation can be extended by a half-integer without changing LIS/LDS.

I showed this is equivalent to the insertion tableau P satisfying certain inequalities. And the count is f_rect(A,B) * g where g counts SYT satisfying those inequalities.

Let me think about g differently. Maybe we can use the "hook walk" or "RSK" interpretation.

In the RSK correspondence, when we append a value x to a permutation, we're doing one step of the growth process. The condition that x does not change LIS/LDS means that the new cell added is (A,B).

The number of permutations that can be extended in this way... maybe it's related to the number of permutations where a specific element (the one corresponding to cell (A,B) in some sense) has a certain property.

Actually, let me think about the recording tableau Q. The pair (P, Q) corresponds to the permutation. P is the insertion tableau, Q is the recording tableau. The shape is λ (rectangle minus corner).

When we append x to get shape μ (full rectangle), we get new pair (P', Q') where P' is P with x inserted, and Q' is Q with N+1 placed at the new cell.

For condition 3 to hold, we need that there exists some x such that P' has shape μ. This is purely about P. Q can be any SYT of shape λ.

So answer = (number of P satisfying constraint) * f^λ.

Now, the constraint on P is equivalent to: when we "reverse" the RSK step, i.e., consider all SYT P' of shape μ (full rectangle), and remove the cell (A,B), we get a SYT P of shape λ. The value at (A,B) in P' is some number k. When we remove (A,B) and perform "reverse insertion" (jeu de taquin), we get back x and P.

Actually, there's a bijection between:
- pairs (P, x) where P is SYT of shape λ and x is a number that inserts to add cell (A,B)
- SYT P' of shape μ

Because given P', we can reverse the insertion of the value at cell (A,B) to recover P and x.

So the number of P for which there exists some x adding (A,B) equals the number of SYT of shape μ (full rectangle) divided by the number of possible x values that add the same cell? No, that's not right.

Actually, for a given P of shape λ, there might be multiple x values that add cell (A,B). And for a given P' of shape μ, there's exactly one way to reverse-insert the value at (A,B) to get (P, x).

So the mapping from (P, x) → P' is a bijection. Therefore, the number of pairs (P, x) such that inserting x into P adds cell (A,B) equals the number of SYT of shape μ, which is f_rect(A,B).

But wait, we don't care about which x; we just need existence of at least one x. So g ≤ number of pairs = f_rect(A,B).

For a given P, how many x values add cell (A,B)? As I analyzed, x must satisfy:
P[1, A-1] < x < P[1, A]

Since P[1, A-1] and P[1, A] are integers, the number of half-integers between them is (P[1, A] - P[1, A-1] - 1) + ... wait.

Actually, x = n + 0.5 for integer n. If P[1, A-1] = k and P[1, A] = k + d (where d ≥ 1), then there are d half-integers between them: k+0.5, k+1.5, ..., k+d-0.5. That's d values.

So the number of x values that work for a given P is P[1, A] - P[1, A-1].

Thus:
Σ_{P satisfying constraints} (P[1, A] - P[1, A-1]) = f_rect(A,B)

Where the sum is over all SYT P of shape λ that satisfy the bumping path constraints (the chain of inequalities). And Σ (P[1, A] - P[1, A-1]) = f_rect(A,B).

Actually, this might be related to the "Erdos–Szekeres" theorem and Greene's theorem. The condition 3 might be equivalent to: the permutation can be embedded in a permutation of size AB with LIS=A and LDS=B.

By Greene's theorem, a permutation of size N has LIS = A and LDS = B iff the RSK shape λ satisfies λ₁ = A and λ₁' = B. For N = AB-1, the only possible shape is the A×B rectangle minus one cell, which must be (A,B). For N = AB, the only shape with λ₁ = A, λ₁' = B is the full A×B rectangle.

Condition 3 says: there's a half-integer that can be appended without changing LIS/LDS. This is equivalent to: the permutation can be extended to a permutation of size AB with the same LIS and LDS. But actually, appending a half-integer gives a sequence of AB distinct numbers, which can be standardized to a permutation of size AB.

Wait: if we append n+0.5 to P (which has values 1..AB-1), we get a sequence of AB distinct numbers. We can then "compress" this to a permutation of {1..AB} by replacing each number with its rank. The LIS and LDS are preserved under order-isomorphism. So condition 3 is equivalent to: there exists a permutation of size AB extending P (in the sense that the relative order of the first AB-1 elements is the same) that has LIS=A and LDS=B.

Equivalently: P can be embedded in a permutation of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B.

Now, the number of permutations of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B is (f_rect(A,B))^2. Each such permutation, when the last element is removed, gives a permutation of size AB-1. Does it always give one with LIS=A, LDS=B? Not necessarily; removing the last element could reduce LIS or LDS. Condition 3 ensures there's at least one way to add an element that preserves LIS/LDS.

So we're counting permutations P of size AB-1 for which there exists at least one extension to a full permutation of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B.

Another way: consider all permutations σ of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B. Remove the last element from each. Some resulting permutations P may appear multiple times (from different σ's). We count those P that appear at least once.

But the condition "there exists n such that appending n+0.5 preserves LIS/LDS" is exactly "P is the prefix of some σ of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B".

Now, the last element of σ can be any value from 1 to AB. When we remove it, we get a permutation of size AB-1. The RSK shape of σ is the full A×B rectangle. When we remove the last element (which corresponds to the cell (A,B) in the recording tableau), the shape becomes λ (rectangle minus (A,B)). 

So the mapping from σ to P is: take the RSK pair (P', Q') of shape μ (full rectangle). Remove the cell containing AB from Q' to get Q of shape λ. Also reverse-insert the value at cell (A,B) from P' to get P and the removed value. Then P is the permutation corresponding to (P, Q).

This is a bijection between σ and (P, Q, x) where x is the value removed from the end. But we're only counting P (forgetting about Q and x and the specific σ).

Actually, each P corresponds to some number of Q's (any SYT of shape λ) times the number of x values that work for P. So the number of σ that reduce to P is: (number of x for P) * f^λ.

Thus: Σ_P (num_x(P) * f^λ) = total σ = (f_rect)^2.

So Σ_P num_x(P) = f_rect.

But num_x(P) = P[1, A] - P[1, A-1] if the chain holds, else 0.

So we have:
Σ_{P: chain holds} (P[1, A] - P[1, A-1]) = f_rect

And g = number of P with chain holding.

This is a sum over SYT of shape λ with certain constraints.

Given a permutation σ of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B, its RSK shape is the full A×B rectangle. Let (P', Q') be its RSK pair.

If we remove the LAST element from σ, we get a permutation P of size AB-1. What are the RSK pair (P, Q) of P?

The recording tableau Q is Q' with the cell containing AB removed (that cell is (A,B) in the full rectangle). The insertion tableau P is P' with the value that was at (A,B) "reverse-inserted" (i.e., we undo the insertion of the last element).

So, Q can be any SYT of shape λ (since any Q' of shape μ restricted to λ gives a SYT Q). And P is determined by P' and the last element.

Now, condition 3 says: there exists some half-integer that can be appended to get a permutation of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B. This is equivalent to: P is the prefix of some σ with the right shape.

When we append n+0.5 to the permutation and it doesn't change LIS/LDS, the new element must land at cell (A,B). In the RSK insertion tableau, the value x = n+0.5 is inserted, following a bumping path to (A,B).

Now, given the insertion tableau P (shape λ), the value x that ends up at (A,B) is uniquely determined by the bumping path: x bumps P[1,A], which bumps P[2,A], ..., which finally lands at (A,B). The value x must satisfy P[1,A-1] < x < P[1,A].

But also, P[1,A] must be such that it bumps P[2,A] (so P[2,A-1] < P[1,A] < P[2,A]), and so on.

So the condition is exactly that this chain of inequalities holds.

Given a permutation σ of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B (shape full rectangle), let's remove the LAST element. This corresponds to removing the cell (A,B) from the recording tableau Q' (which contains AB at that cell). We also reverse-insert the value at cell (A,B) from P'. 

The resulting permutation P has shape λ (rectangle minus corner) if and only if the removed element was at position AB (the end). Wait, any permutation of size AB with shape A×B, when you remove the last element, gives a permutation of size AB-1. What is its shape?

It could be either λ (rectangle minus corner) or some other shape. Actually, by Greene's theorem, the shape of P is obtained from the shape of σ by removing one cell. Since σ has shape A×B (full rectangle), removing one cell gives either the rectangle minus a corner cell. But which corner?

When we remove the last element of σ, we are removing the entry at position AB from the permutation. In the RSK recording tableau Q', this is the cell containing AB. The shape of Q' is the full rectangle. AB is at some outer corner of the shape. Since the full rectangle has only one outer corner that can be removed while keeping the shape a Young diagram (the cell (A,B)), AB must be at (A,B). 

Wait, is that true? In a standard Young tableau of shape A×B, the value AB (the largest) must be at an outer corner of the shape. The outer corners of a rectangle are: (1, A+1) (right of row 1), (B+1, 1) (below column 1), and (A,B) (the bottom-right cell). But (1, A+1) and (B+1, 1) are outside the rectangle. The only outer corner INSIDE the rectangle is (A,B). But AB could also be at (1, A) if A < ...? No, (1,A) has a cell below it (2,A), so it's not an outer corner. In a rectangle, the only inner corner (which becomes an outer corner when we consider removing cells) is (A,B). 

Actually, in a SYT of shape A×B, the value AB is placed at some cell that is a "removable corner" of the shape. For a rectangle, the only removable corner is (A,B). So AB must be at (A,B). 

Therefore, when we remove the last element from σ, we remove cell (A,B) from Q', and the resulting shape is λ (rectangle minus (A,B)). So EVERY permutation of size AB with LIS=A, LDS=B, when the last element is removed, yields a permutation of size AB-1 with LIS=A, LDS=B.

So ALL (f_rect)^2 permutations of size AB reduce to permutations of size AB-1 with the same LIS/LDS. But some of those reduced permutations may be the same (multiple size-AB permutations reduce to the same size-AB-1 permutation).

Indeed, for a given P of size AB-1 with LIS=A, LDS=B, how many σ of size AB reduce to it? That's exactly the number of x values that can be appended to P to preserve LIS/LDS. If that number is zero, then P is not counted by condition 3.

So condition 3 selects exactly those P that are the prefix of at least one σ.

And the sum over all P (with condition 3) of (number of x that work) = total number of σ = (f_rect)^2.

Since number of x = P[1,A] - P[1,A-1], we have:
Σ_P (P[1,A] - P[1,A-1]) = (f_rect)^2 = f^2.

Actually, C(2B-2, B-1) / C(2B, B) = 
= [(2B-2)! / ((B-1)!(B-1)!)] / [(2B)! / (B!B!)]
= (2B-2)! * B! * B! / ((B-1)! * (B-1)! * (2B)!)
= B^2 / (2B*(2B-1))
= B / (2*(2B-1))

So C(2B-2, B-1) / C(2B, B) = B / (2*(2B-1)).

And R(2,B) = (B+1)/(2*(2B-1)).

So R(2,B) / [C(2B-2, B-1) / C(2B, B)] = (B+1)/B.

Thus R(2,B) = C(2B-2, B-1) / C(2B, B) * (B+1)/B.

But more simply:
R(2,B) = (B+1) / (2*(2B-1)).

Let me think more deeply about the combinatorial structure.

The shape λ is the A×B rectangle minus cell (A,B). The number of SYT of this shape is f_rect(A,B).

The chain constraints involve only the cells in columns A-1 and A. Specifically, for rows 1..B-1, we need:
P[i+1, A-1] < P[i, A]   (for i=1..B-1)

And P[B-1, A] > P[B, A-1] which is the same as above for i=B-1 (as I showed).

So the constraints are: for each i from 1 to B-1, the value at (i+1, A-1) is less than the value at (i, A).

Equivalently, if we list the values in column A-1 (rows 1..B) and column A (rows 1..B-1), we need:
P[2, A-1] < P[1, A]
P[3, A-1] < P[2, A]
...
P[B, A-1] < P[B-1, A]

Now, note that columns A-1 and A form a "2 × B" rectangle with the corner (2,B) removed (since row B of column A doesn't exist). Actually, it's a shape (2, 2, ..., 2, 1) with B rows, where the first B-1 rows have 2 cells and the last row has 1 cell.

The constraints say that within this sub-shape, the values must satisfy the "staggered" inequalities.

Wait, this sub-shape of size 2B-1 is itself a Young diagram (a truncated 2×B rectangle). The chain constraints impose additional order relations beyond the standard SYT constraints.

But note: the SYT constraints already impose that within this sub-shape, values increase along rows and columns. The chain constraints add cross-relations.

This is reminiscent of the "Kreweras complement" or the "evacuation" operation on SYT.

- P' = insertion of x into P
- Q' = Q with N+1 added at the new cell

So the new cell added to Q is determined by the insertion of x into P. The recording tableau Q just gets N+1 at that cell.

Therefore, condition 3 depends only on P, not Q. My earlier analysis stands.

Insert into P\n        schensted_insert(P, x)\n        # Record in Q at same shape\n        # Build Q by tracking where new cell was added\n        # Actually, standard RSK: Q gets the index (1-indexed) at the new cell\n        # insertion tableau\n    Q = []  # recording tableau\n    for idx, x in enumerate(perm):\n        # Insert x into P, track bumping path\n        r = 0\n        val = x\n        while r < len(P):\n            row = P[r]\n            j = bisect.bisect_left(row, val)\n            if j == len(row):\n                row.append(val)\n                # where C_n is the n-th Catalan number.

Outer corners (cells that can be added while maintaining a valid Young diagram):
- (1, A+1): extending row 1 beyond column A. This would increase λ₁ to A+1, i.e., LIS becomes A+1.
- (B+1, 1): extending column 1 beyond row B. This would increase λ₁' to B+1, i.e., LDS becomes B+1.
- (A, B): the missing corner. Adding it gives the full A×B rectangle, with λ₁ = A, λ₁' = B unchanged.
- Are there any other outer corners? For a partition shape, outer corners are positions (i,j) such that (i-1,j) and (i,j-1) are in the shape (or i=1 or j=1). For λ:
  - (1, A+1): (1, A) is in λ, so yes.
  - (B+1, 1): (B, 1) is in λ, so yes.
  - (A, B): (A-1, B) and (A, B-1) are in λ, so yes.

So (B, A) has left neighbor (B, A-1) in λ and top neighbor (B-1, A) in λ. So (B, A) IS an outer corner! But that's the missing cell (A,B) (row B, column A). So that's just the missing corner.

So the only outer corners are (1, A+1), (B+1, 1), and (B, A). Only (B, A) (the missing corner) preserves λ₁ = A and λ₁' = B.

Maybe I'm missing something about the condition. Let me re-derive the chain more carefully.

The insertion of x into row 1 of the tableau P:
- If x > all elements in row 1, x is placed at the end, extending row 1 by one cell. The new cell is (1, A+1). This increases LIS.
- If x < P[1, 1], x bumps P[1, 1]. Then P[1, 1] goes to row 2, and may bump an element there, etc. The new cell ends up in column 1 of some row (possibly extending the first column). This increases LDS.
- If P[1, j-1] < x < P[1, j] for some j, x bumps P[1, j]. The bumped element goes to row 2, etc.

For the new cell to be (A, B) (adding the missing corner), we need:
- x bumps P[1, A] (so x must be between P[1, A-1] and P[1, A]).
- P[1, A] bumps P[2, A] (so P[2, A-1] < P[1, A] < P[2, A]).
- ...
- P[B-2, A] bumps P[B-1, A] (so P[B-1, A-1] < P[B-2, A] < P[B-1, A]).
- P[B-1, A] is placed at the end of row B (column A), since row B has length A-1.

This is exactly the chain I derived.

But wait: what if the bumping path goes through column A in some rows but not others? Could the new cell still be (A, B)?

For the new cell to be at column A of row B, the element that lands at row B must be greater than all elements in row B (which has A-1 elements). So it must be greater than P[B, A-1]. This is the constraint P[B-1, A] > P[B, A-1].

The element that lands at row B is bumped from row B-1. For it to be placed at column A of row B, it must be greater than P[B, A-1]. It could be that the bump in row B-1 happened at column A (giving the element P[B-1, A]) or at an earlier column. If the bump happened at column j < A, then the bumped element would be P[B-1, j]. This element goes to row B, and for it to be placed at column A, it would need to be greater than all elements in row B, i.e., greater than P[B, A-1]. But then P[B-1, j] < P[B-1, A-1] (since row B-1 is increasing), and P[B-1, A-1] < P[B, A-1] (column). So P[B-1, j] < P[B-1, A-1] < P[B, A-1], meaning P[B-1, j] < P[B, A-1]. So it can't be placed at column A. Therefore the bump must have occurred at column A of row B-1.

By induction, all bumps must be at column A. So the chain is necessary.
